#119. Microsoft says services fully restored after day-long global outage

Products such as Microsoft Teams and Outlook have been out of service for much of Friday

Microsoft has said its services have been fully restored after a worldwide outage affected several of its tools, causing disruption.

The tech firm said early on Saturday morning that efforts to address the underlying problem had been completed and customers using Outlook, Teams and Copilot should no longer be affected.

The outage hit more than 70 countries, disrupting everything from business operations to government services and personal users. More than 430 million people use the affected products worldwide.

Microsoft apologised for the technical issue, which it said was introduced during a "network capacity upgrade" and was not caused by a cyber-attack or an artificial intelligence agent.

Experts said the event was a "stark reminder" of how heavily businesses and vital services rely on a small group of big tech providers.

Users began reporting they were having trouble accessing Microsoft 365 products on Thursday.

By Friday, the far-reaching issue was affecting institutions from the Japanese government and the Philippines Supreme Court to the Australian spy agency, and trains, hospitals and courts in North America.

Services were beginning to recover by Friday afternoon UK time, nearly 24 hours after the issues first began to emerge.

Why is Microsoft's outage lasting so long and will emails be lost?

Courts, spies and MPs: How the Microsoft outage has been felt around the world

In the UK, some parts of the NHS were affected, as were some schools and police forces.

UK cyber minister Liz Lloyd said the incident had "limited" impact on critical frontline services such as 999 calls and that there had been no disruption to the transport network.

In Canada, however, the national rail network said passengers may not be able to receive email updates about their trips, and hospitals across the country also had issues with emails.

In the US, staff members from the House of Representatives told Politico it had set back an entire day of legislative work as everything was on Microsoft's Outlook.

Singapore's government acknowledged some of its systems had been impacted, and in India, the outage disrupted work for many on Friday.

In Turkey, advisers and officials attending the COP31 climate summit in Antalya were left frustrated by being unable to access Outlook.

The issues also affected smaller businesses and individuals.

Pete says his business has been brought to a standstill

Peter Pentz, a winemaker in South Africa, said his entire business had been effectively brought to a "standstill".

His winery's communication with exports was also brought to a standstill, he said, and "critical production work" had been halted as the Cape West Coast winery "relies on these systems for parts of our day-to-day operations".

Georgia from Staffordshire in England said she had been due to complete her first house purchase after waiting eight months to get to this point - but the outage has delayed that.

"The solicitor mentioned there may be a delay due to not being able to contact other agencies," she said.

"We're not the only one completing today - there are hundreds of houses and for them to ring everyone in time is unfeasible."

Microsoft first acknowledged users in the Americas were unable to access Exchange Online mailboxes at 17:54 GMT on Thursday, and shortly after warned the disruption was possibly affecting Microsoft 365 globally.

It launched an investigation and engineers began working to restore services, but by 04:36 on Friday Microsoft was reporting only a "partial recovery" in some parts of the world.

By 13:32, the tech giant said there had been "broader improvements" in North America, "sustained improvements" across much of Europe and "encouraging recovery signals" in the Asia Pacific region.

Microsoft said it restored services gradually due to the scale of the problem and to help ensure services remained stable once they were up and running again.

"We are deeply sorry for the disruption this incident has caused our customers and partners," a Microsoft spokesperson said.

"We know the trust they place in our services and take that responsibility extremely seriously."

A full post-incident report will be released once Microsoft has completed a review.

Experts said that while workers may have been happy to not have email access for a working day, the incident showed the dependence many organisations have on these cloud-based services.

"The global Microsoft outage is a stark reminder of how heavily we rely on a handful of big tech providers," said Rob Demain, chief executive of cybersecurity firm e2e-assure.

Matt Saunders, field chief technology officer at tech consultancy Adaptavist, said it would be challenging to mitigate against a similar problem.

"Classic IT disaster planning strategies suggest using a second service for resilience, but in practice, it's almost impossible for a business to function without tools such as Microsoft," he said.

"In the wake of this outage, organisations must have a plan in place for how to work without such tools, for when incidents like this occur."

#118. Analysis: Companies love a ‘rockstar’ CEO. Until they depend on one

When Jensen Huang quit, it struck me how quickly the conversation shifted from his departure to whether Nvidia could manage without him.

It wasn't about whether the company had the technology, customers or talent to keep growing, but whether anyone else could do what Huang had been doing for the business. That made me wonder: did investors believe in Nvidia, or just Huang?

The company is hardly short of capable people. Interim CEO and Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress has been with Nvidia since 2013. Nvidia has experienced engineers, established customer relationships and a leading position in the artificial intelligence chip market.

But, listening to guests on CNBC this week, I noticed how much of their concern was about the influence Huang had built outside Nvidia.

On Friday, just a day after Huang quit, Dylan Patel of SemiAnalysis told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" how Huang would persuade executives at smaller companies that make critical components to commit capacity to Nvidia. This helped it secure "the majority of supply chain capacity," he said.

Without Huang, competitors could try to persuade those suppliers to allocate more capacity to them, Patel argued. And so the big question is, how much of Nvidia's advantage comes from its technology and how much comes from the relationships Huang built?

There are obvious benefits to elevating CEOs to be rockstars. They can attract investors, open doors and give a company a new identity. A charismatic leader attracts attention, reassures investors and makes a company's strategy easier to sell.

Huang was particularly good at this. He helped shape the conversation about where AI was heading and made Nvidia central to it. Donning his trademark leather jacket, Huang took to stages around the world, courting world leaders to lay out his vision and explain why Nvidia would be at its center.

Before Huang resigned, Eric Ross told "Squawk Box Asia" that Huang "is the company."

That may sound like an endorsement, but it also helps explain why his departure has raised so many questions.

Bernstein's Stacy Rasgon made an interesting distinction on the same program, also before Huang's departure. Nvidia had capable executives who could keep the business running, he said. What was harder to replace was Huang's vision.

"They need a visionary like that," he said.

But for a company whose valuation reflects not just what it sells but expectations of what AI could become, replacing that vision may prove harder than replacing the CEO.

Duncan Clark pointed to another part of Huang's job. He had become a bridge between Silicon Valley, Asia and Washington, and one of the most prominent voices for the wider AI industry.

"I don't think you can replace him with one individual," Clark said on "Access Middle East" on Friday. Replacing him with multiple people is "going to take time, and it won't be the same," he added.

That distinction may be important. Nvidia's board has begun a search for a permanent CEO and said it will consider both internal and external candidates. But whoever takes the job may not personally assume every role Huang had come to play around the company. Some of his responsibilities and relationships could end up being spread across Nvidia's broader leadership team.

For now, the board is emphasizing continuity. New chair Harvey Jones said Kress is backed by an experienced leadership team and that Nvidia expected its operations, customer commitments and product roadmap to continue without interruption. Kress, meanwhile, pointed to Nvidia's people and leadership team as strengths as the company moves forward.

Other boards will be watching, particularly those that have allowed their CEOs to become the face of everything their companies do.

When a charismatic leader is the only person outsiders hear from, it becomes harder to convince them that anyone else can take over. There is also the question of accountability.

Clark described Nvidia's situation as a governance failure. And my colleague, "Squawk Box Europe" anchor Steve Sedgwick, raised a similar point on Friday about how corporate America has approached ESG.

Much of the political debate has focused on its environmental component, Steve said. President Donald Trump has repeatedly attacked environmental and climate policies using phrases such as "green scam." But Steve questioned whether the governance element has been overlooked, particularly at some of America's biggest technology companies.

"When you have a 'Rockstar' CEO you need way more governance, NOT less," he said, citing his recent conversations with CEOs and senior executives.

His point was that powerful leaders can come to believe the normal rules don't apply to them. Without strong governance to challenge them and prepare for their departure, the consequences for a company can be far greater.

Linda Ford, chief executive of The Chartered Governance Institute in the U.K., said Nvidia showed what could happen "when a company's leadership is built around one person." She noted that Nvidia had never previously had a board chair, adding: "A high-profile leader can be an asset, but the board needs to work hard to protect its independence and provide the scrutiny shareholders are entitled to expect."

And that gets to the bigger issue. How easily can a board challenge a CEO who has delivered extraordinary success, particularly when investors have come to see that person as indispensable?

Companies spend years putting leaders in front of investors and customers and making them central to the business. But how much effort goes into making sure the company can function without them?

Nvidia now has to show that the business Huang built can stand on its own. Other boards might want to ask themselves whether they could say the same.

#116. Feds arrest top official of healthcare company, alleging illegal drug importation

U.S. authorities are accusing the two top officials of a Canadian company of operating a long-running scheme to illegally import prescription drugs into the U.S., according to court records filed Tuesday.

John Thomas Howard, treasurer and a director of the company, CANARX, was arrested Sunday on smuggling and conspiracy charges, according to the records.

Bob Howard, his brother, who is president and a director of CANARX, is also wanted in the federal investigation, according to an affidavit from Dustin Dobbs, a special agent with the Food and Drug Administration's Office of Criminal Investigations. He has not yet been taken into custody.

Besides conspiracy and smuggling, the arrest warrant said John Thomas Howard was charged with introducing misbranded drugs into interstate commerce and illegal drug importation. No plea has been filed.

CANARX was featured in a CNBC investigation last year that revealed how the company and others known as alternative funding programs, or AFPs, are becoming more pervasive around the U.S. as drug costs skyrocket. The investigation found these companies have penetrated the country's healthcare system by offering high-cost specialty drugs at more affordable prices through private and public employers. In some cases, employers are requiring their staff to use an AFP.

AFPs often obtain the medications overseas and ship them directly to patients, a practice that federal authorities have said is illegal and poses safety risks. CNBC traced the trail of drugs from abroad to the U.S. via a supply chain that the pharmaceutical companies producing the medications have not authorized.

On its website, CANARX, founded in 2002 and based in Windsor, Ontario, Canada, described its business as the first international prescription provider for the U.S. and the safest such program in the industry. The company said it sources its medications from Canada, the United Kingdom, Australia and New Zealand.

But the affidavit said that the FDA's investigation found "there is probable cause to believe that (CANARX) illegally imported or caused the illegal importation of prescription drugs into the United States …."

The affidavit does not specify which medications the agency believes were illegally imported. The criminal complaint covers the period from Feb. 22, 2017, through April 15, 2026.

The company's general counsel, Joseph Morris, told CNBC in a statement: "The Howards deny all the charges against them, will defend vigorously, and expect to be vindicated in court."

The company has been under FDA scrutiny for several years. In 2023, the agency seized medication being shipped by CANARX that it says was potentially misbranded and counterfeit, according to an email sent to a patient that was obtained by CNBC.

And in 2019, the FDA sent a warning letter to the company, saying it was operating "in a manner that substitutes the FDA-approved drugs prescribed by the U.S. healthcare provider with unapproved drugs." Morris, in response to that warning, argued CANARX was not misleading patients, but agreed for the company to stop importing drugs that need to be administered by a doctor.

In an interview as part of the CNBC investigation, Morris said last year that the medicines shipped to U.S. patients come from highly regulated countries. He said CANARX was operating legally.

The central question for AFPs is the legality of importing prescription drugs into the U.S. for personal use. The FDA allows Americans to import medications for individual use in some cases if the drug is not available domestically. That is the basis on which the AFPs built their models for shipping medicines directly to patients.

But an FDA spokesperson told CNBC last year that "in most circumstances, a person cannot import prescription drugs from other countries into the U.S. ... as substitutes for FDA-approved drugs."

In a separate civil lawsuit that is pending, Gilead Sciences accused CANARX and other companies of illegally importing Biktarvy, its medication to treat HIV.

In a statement to CNBC on Wednesday, a Gilead spokesperson said the company "has taken strong legal action to protect patients and the integrity of the U.S. medicines supply chain. We welcome the government's efforts to hold accountable those whose conduct may put patients at risk."

CANARX has not responded to the Gilead lawsuit.

— CNBC's Paige Tortorelli and Melissa Lee contributed to this report

#114. Microsoft apologizes for massive outage that took down Outlook and other 365 services

Microsoft issued an apology Friday for a wide-ranging outage that shut down Outlook and other Microsoft 365 products, as the company worked to restore service nearly 24 hours after connectivity problems began.

"We are deeply sorry for the disruption this incident has caused our customers and partners. We know the trust they place in our services and take that responsibility extremely seriously," a Microsoft spokesperson said Friday.

On Saturday, Microsoft said it had addressed the underlying issue and restored service to the remaining impacted customers and regions.

The connectivity disruption stemmed from "an issue during an earlier network capacity upgrade," Microsoft said in a Friday statement. The software giant added that the underlying cause of the outage had been addressed and that they were still working to restore services to some customers.

Engineers scrambled to solve connectivity problems that began shortly before 1 p.m. ET on Thursday.

Email and calendar service Outlook, along with multiple Microsoft 365 services such as Sharepoint and Copilot, were largely down during the morning hours in the U.S. on Friday and throughout the Asian and European business days. Numerous external services that connect with Microsoft products were also hit. Users began to regain access to email and other services Friday morning.

In an update around 10:30 a.m. ET on Friday, Microsoft noted "encouraging recovery progress" and said that "service availability and email delivery performance [was] improving across a growing portion of affected infrastructure" as Outlook and other Microsoft 365 tools began to come back online.

In an earlier update Friday morning, Microsoft said that email queues were "approaching normal levels," but that intermittent issues were expected as recovery efforts continue. It had previously given updates by region, with Asia Pacific services among the first to start normalizing.

Engineering teams have been taking affected systems offline for fixes and bringing them back online gradually stages. They are prioritizing efforts to preserve emails delayed during the recovery process and reduce email queues and backlogs, extending the timeout window, whereby delayed emails continue to attempt delivery, to 48 hours to help protect messages.

Microsoft says it has not detected any malicious activity causing the outage, which it initially blamed on a traffic surge in part of its infrastructure and issues with a component responsible for processing server health. The company said it would issue a full report once its assessment of the outage was complete.

The company said in its initial updates on Thursday that the connectivity problems related to infrastructure "elevated load across multiple regions," caused by a "number of servers being unexpectedly unavailable; straining the remaining infrastructure." Some servers were failing automated health checks, it said.

Microsoft shares traded largely flat Friday.

Governments and businesses across the U.S., Asia-Pacific and Europe said they were monitoring the situation, which was causing widespread disruption.

The U.K.'s National Health Service said the outage was affecting some of its services but that patients should attend appointments as normal. Calls "may be subject to long waits" as a result of disruption to the organization, a hospital group serving South West London and the English county of Surrey said on X.

Earnings for Swiss luxury goods giant Richemont were slightly delayed on Friday due to the outage. Electric truck maker Workhorse Group, which was expected to report third-quarter results on Nov. 12, rescheduled the release to Nov. 16, citing business disruptions caused by the global Microsoft outage.

U.S. Rep. Josh Gottheimer, D-N.J., told CNBC's "The Exchange" that he asked for a briefing on the outage. Gottheimer worked at Microsoft prior to serving in Congress.

"Is there something that went on that's nefarious here, or is this just something else? These outages occur in a lot of the companies, but the size of it and the length of it and the timing of it," Gottheimer said.

Air India said it had "temporarily rerouted" emails so it could continue to receive customer messages, adding that its flight operations were not affected.

Australian Home Affairs and Cyber Security Minister Tony Burke said in a press conference that Microsoft "believe they are dealing in some ways similar to what we saw with respect to CrowdStrike where there is an update that has gone awry," Reuters reported.

A fault with an update issued by cybersecurity company CrowdStrike caused one of the world's biggest IT system outages in 2024, hitting airlines, banks and more.

Microsoft reported in 2025 that Copilot, its collection of AI assistants and tools, had more than 100 million monthly active users.

The incident highlights a business "resilience and dependency issue that has been decades in the making," James Owen, managing partner of Digital Risks at consultancy Control Risks, said by phone.

"We have become, organizationally in the private sector and more broadly, increasingly dependent on a smaller segment of technology and infrastructure as critical to daily life and business activities."

"Organizations are becoming more aware through a geopolitical lens about what technologies they adopt, whether their services are provided by firms in the U.S., or China, or elsewhere, but this shows the importance of proper recovery planning in the case of an IT outage. That could be manual backups, protocols that do not require internet connection, investing in diverse suppliers and crisis management planning rehearsals," he said.

Amazon Web Services and Google users also reported outages on the site on Thursday.

Amazon wrote on X that services were "operating normally" and an "unrelated disruption elsewhere on the internet is prompting some inaccurate news reports." The company referred users to its health dashboard for "accurate data."

At 4 p.m. ET Thursday, Microsoft said it had "validated an alternative service health check method" that brought some affected servers back online.

"We're now applying this change more broadly," Microsoft wrote. "We're also preparing automated recovery systems to continually detect and restart unresponsive servers, to maintain service stability while this change progresses."

#111. Meet the seven co-founders of Anthropic

Four researchers, a former journalist, a public policymaker, and an engineer founded Anthropic in 2021. Six years later, the artificial intelligence company is about to go public, with trading expected to begin on Nov. 19, in what will be one of the biggest IPOs on record.

Those seven co-founders are poised to each be worth many billions of dollars.

At the end of 2020, siblings Dario and Daniela Amodei were vice presidents at OpenAI, the company that kicked off the generative AI frenzy with the launch of ChatGPT. But the two had grown frustrated with their employer over its approach to AI safety. Fearing a world where AI models' capabilities would progress faster than our understanding of them, the duo launched Anthropic early the next year.

They took a handful of OpenAI colleagues with them: Tom Brown, the company's research engineering lead on GPT-3; Jack Clark, a policy director; Jared Kaplan, a contract research consultant; and Sam McCandlish and Chris Olah, both members of OpenAI's technical staff.

They were all deeply concerned about OpenAI's ambitions to focus on commercialization, and they now stand to be some of the first to cash in on the AI boom.

"They really started Anthropic not because they were hoping to get rich, but they started Anthropic because they like wanted to shape this really important thing in the world, and they thought that other people might do it worse than them," said Jeffrey Ladish, who joined Anthropic as a contract safety engineer in September 2021, and now serves as the executive director of AI safety institute Palisade Research.

Collectively the co-founders own about 10% of Anthropic. Each has about 240,000,000 class A shares, or about a 1.4% stake.

During Anthropic's most recent funding round, those shares — valued at $58.90 each — amounted to about $14 billion in value for each co-founder. If Anthropic's IPO reaches its target of $70 to $78 per share, or a market valuation of nearly $1.4 trillion, it would push those stakes to $17 billion to $18 billion.

"It's extremely hard not to be influenced by your incentives," Ladish said. "You have investors, and money is not just like an end; it's also a means."

Anthropic did not respond to requests for comment.

The face of Anthropic is CEO Dario Amodei. He holds the most shares of the bunch: 245,259,100 including shares subject to voting proxies.

Amodei, 43, didn't get his start in business. He comes from the world of research, obtaining a doctorate in computational neuroscience from Princeton University in 2011. He then went to work at Stanford University as a postdoctoral scholar from 2011 to 2014.

Even in the private sector, Amodei's experience was focused on research. He worked at China's Baidu, leading research on speech recognition from 2014 to 2015, followed by a stint at Google Brain working on deep learning and neural networks, foundational scientific frameworks for today's large language models. He joined OpenAI in July 2016.

On the Big Technology podcast in July 2025, Amodei said he got into AI to solve the "almost intractable" and "slow moving" problems of biology.

"I think my reason to start a company was that I had worked at other companies and I just didn't feel like the way those companies were run was really oriented towards trying to have that impact," he said, adding that the desire for impact was often used by companies as an insincere ploy to recruit talent.

At OpenAI, Amodei is credited with co-inventing reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), alongside Anthropic co-founder, Tom Brown. RLHF is a fine-tuning method used to align model outputs with human needs. That could mean training a model to speak politely or to not offer violent or harmful solutions to problems.

Four years her brother's junior, Daniela Amodei, 39, serves as the president and board chair of Anthropic. The Amodeis are both San Francisco natives, growing up in the Mission District, just a few miles away from Anthropic's current headquarters.

The pair were teenagers during the height of the dot-com bubble and its subsequent fallout, both attending Lowell High School, a magnet school and one of the most prestigious public schools in the country. After graduating, Daniela went on to study English literature and politics at the University of California, Santa Cruz, earning her degree in 2009.

Daniela started her career in politics, working for Matt Cartwright, a Democrat representing what was then Pennsylvania's 17th congressional district, and then moved into tech, taking on various roles in risk management at Stripe for five years.

In a June interview with Stanford's Graduate School of Business, she said her experience in politics, specifically campaigns, naturally led her to startups and Silicon Valley.

"I was like, 'Wow, a small number of people that work really hard, who are young and driven, can really change the world,'" she said.

Anthropic's chief compute officer, Tom Brown, 39, is perhaps the most entrepreneurial of the bunch. After graduating from the Massachusetts Institute of Technology with a Master of Engineering in 2010, Brown went on to found two startups, MoPub and Grouper.

MoPub was a mobile advertising business acquired by Twitter in 2013. There he served as founding engineer in 2011. Grouper, where Brown served as co-founder and technology chief, was a Y Combinator-backed group dating app.

After Grouper, Brown pivoted to research, sharing on Y Combinator's Lightcone podcast in August 2025 that he was self-taught on machine learning research, a daunting task since he received a B- in linear algebra at university.

"I kind of flip-flopped on it for six months trying to build up courage to" make the switch, he said.

Alongside co-inventing RLHF, as a researcher at OpenAI, Brown led the training efforts on GPT-3, a model that marked a historic leap from GPT-2, going from 1.5 billion parameters to 175 billion.

Jack Clark, 37, the head of public benefit at Anthropic, left his career as a journalist in 2016 to work at OpenAI. Clark is now also the leader of the Anthropic Institute, the company's internal think tank that explores AI's social and economic impacts.

A native of the U.K., Clark grew up in the seaside town of Brighton and later studied English literature and creative writing at the University of East Anglia. He then went on to write for publications including ZDNet UK, The Register, and Bloomberg.

In an email to a colleague at Bloomberg at the time, Clark said his decision to leave journalism was because he had "never believed in something strongly before" and that he needed to join OpenAI "and live with the consequences, which I hope are good."

Kaplan, 42, Anthropic's chief science officer, still holds a position as an associate professor in the department of physics and astronomy at Johns Hopkins University. The theoretical physicist got his PhD in physics from Harvard in 2009, working for four years after as a postdoctoral fellow at the SLAC National Accelerator Laboratory, a particle physics research facility run by Stanford and funded by the Department of Energy.

Kaplan co-pioneered scaling laws, or the notion that large language models get exponentially better when trained on larger and larger data sets, alongside Dario Amodei. He also pioneered "constitutional AI," or a method of aligning models by using a list of safety principles and policies. His day-to-day work concerns safety assessments for models, responsible scaling policy at the company, and existential risks.

In an interview with The Guardian in December 2025, Kaplan noted that the decision over whether or not to let AI "recursively self improve," or write the code to enhance itself without a human in the loop, would be "in some ways the ultimate risk, because it's kind of like letting AI kind of go," and that he expects this risk will be taken sometime between 2027 and 2030.

Anthropic's chief architect, Sam McCandlish, 36, came to OpenAI as a fellow focused on AI safety. He also led early research on coding tool Codex, which now constitutes a significant portion of OpenAI's revenue.

McCandlish claims he and the rest of the Anthropic co-founders were not following an entrepreneurial spirit when they charted out on their own. In a December 2024 video from Anthropic's YouTube channel, he said, "None of us wanted to found a company. We just felt like it was our duty."

McCandlish, like Kaplan, also came from the world of academia, completing his PhD in theoretical and mathematical physics at Stanford in 2017 and working at Boston University as a postdoctoral fellow the year afterward. His work focused on quantum gravity and tensor networks.

Now, his work focuses on pre-training, or the first step of training a large language model on swaths of data, among other things.

Chris Olah, 34, heads Anthropic's interpretability research, a branch of study in machine learning that attempts to understand how a large language model "thinks" — or rather, which data inputs and reasoning methods it uses to arrive at its outputs.

Like McCandlish, Olah was not originally keen on starting a company. In the same December 2024 YouTube video, Olah said, "I was arguing for a long time that we should do a nonprofit instead, and just focus on safety research."

Olah had a nontraditional path to Anthropic, dropping out of the University of Toronto after one year and working for short stints as a developer at the government agency Environment Canada and software startup Xelerance.

His career truly took off when he was awarded a Thiel Fellowship in 2012, which he used to pursue research pertaining to 3D printers for two years. He then joined Google Brain, first as an intern and then as a researcher, before moving to OpenAI in 2018.

While together the seven founders may only own about 10% of total equity, they will have a majority vote. Founder LLC, the legal entity set up that owns a single Class F share, gives Anthropic's co-founders a 50.1% majority voting control over the company. While Benjamin Mann, a member of Anthropic's technical staff and also an ex-OpenAI employee, is often called a co-founder, he is not an owner of Founder LLC.

The co-founders are expected to collectively sell over 20 million shares during the IPO, resulting in roughly $250 million in cash per founder, according to Anthropic's prospectus.

They plan to dedicate 80% of the wealth from their personal Anthropic equity to philanthropy, according to the IPO filing, though the pledge isn't legally binding. Anthropic is also setting aside 5% of its shares to form a new public benefit fund — to finance nonprofits, research, and potentially direct community grants — with potential to grow the size of the fund as the company itself grows.

#110. Scout Motors has 212,000 vehicle reservations as $2 billion South Carolina plant nears completion

BLYTHEWOOD, S.C. — Electric vehicle startup Scout Motors has notched roughly 212,000 reservations for its upcoming EVs as the company nears completion of its new $2 billion plant here, CEO Scott Keogh told CNBC.

The reservations are for the Volkswagen-backed company's Terra pickup truck and Traveler SUV, which are expected to start full production next year before deliveries begin to customers in 2028.

Reservations do not necessarily translate to sales, as the U.S. automotive industry has learned during the past decade, especially with highly anticipated all-electric trucks such as the Ford F-150 Lightning, Tesla Cybertruck and others that garnered strong interest that didn't lead to meaningful numbers of purchases.

Keogh said he expects Scout could roughly double those reservations by the time customer production begins, but he said the more important thing is to convert early interest into actual sales as production nears.

"It's been a consistent drumbeat," Keogh said during an interview at Scout's plant site outside Columbia. "I think we could easily be up over 400,000 with reservations without doing too much effort. The big thing for us is how much of the initial production can we 100% presell."

Keogh said achieving an order book of 12 to 18 months "would be great" and is something he thinks the company can do before production. Scout on Thursday said plant construction is "in its final phase."

Scout's vehicles will be offered as all-electric versions as well as electric hybrid variants, known as extended-range electric vehicles, or EREVs, that include an internal combustion engine as a backup generator for the electric motors and batteries.

Scout, a former American vehicle brand from 1961 to 1980, was expected to exclusively offer EVs in a bid for the German automaker to expand its presence in the U.S. However, slower-than-expected adoption of EVs and higher costs led the company to decide to offer EREVs as well.

Keogh said extended-range and SUV models are dominating reservations thus far, with roughly 87% choosing EREV models and 75% being SUVs.

The CEO also said the company is drawing interest from its target markets, which include owners of off-road vehicles such as the Ford Bronco and Jeep Wrangler as well as full-size pickup trucks and U.S. EV leader Tesla.

Given the interest signaled by reservations, Keogh said the company is expected to launch with the EREVs first, followed by the EVs. He also said the company plans to launch with mid-level models first, a deviation from many companies that begin with their highest-priced models.

Both the Traveler and Terra are expected to start at under $60,000. The EREV models are expected to feature more than 500 miles of range, according to the company, compared with 300 miles of range for the all-electric models.

"The No.1 thing for me is I want to go get the best volume as soon as possible," Keogh said. "In my mind, it's how quick can you get to 100,000 units? It's at 100,000 units where you can start to punch through."

His comments come months after Rivian Automotive received criticism online for launching its R2 SUV with its highest-priced models first, despite that being a common practice across the automotive industry.

Keogh said Scout's South Carolina plant is about 85% complete, but supporting infrastructure and a $300 million supplier park continue to be installed.

Reservations for Scout's vehicles initially opened in October 2024, when it revealed the Traveler and Terra, which feature sleek, yet rugged, exteriors that pay some homage to the brand's historic models.

#105. Trump doesn’t plan to raise Nvidia, spy issue during China trip: U.S. officials

President Donald Trump is not expected to raise allegations of a Chinese intelligence operation involving former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang during his upcoming meeting with Chinese President Xi Jinping, senior U.S. officials said Friday.

Officials also said that Elon Musk, the CEO of Tesla and SpaceX, will accompany Trump on the trip to Shenzhen, China, for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit on Nov. 18 and 19.

The White House, earlier Friday, said Trump plans to have a three-way summit there with Xi and Russian President Vladimir Putin.

Jensen Huang's relationship with the suspected spy, Alice Huang, who is not related to him, has raised concerns that a Singapore-based company where she was an executive diverted restricted Nvidia chips to China.

"The situation with Nvidia should not really have any bearing on the trip," an official said on the press call, speaking on the condition that they not be identified.

"In all of our discussions and negotiations with the Chinese, we do not negotiate American export controls ... and we don't expect it to figure at all in any of our conversations with the Chinese while we're there."

Asked if Trump would raise the allegations against Alice Huang to Chinese leader Xi Jinping, the official said, "The focus of a meeting with President Xi, at least on the economic side, is going to be about implementation of the trade agreement."

"When it comes to some of these national security matters and examinations and investigations that are going on in the U.S., [that is] probably not something that we're going to read the Chinese in on at this moment," the official said.

Another official said, "I don't expect the president to raise it — the Alice Huang issue."

China has called the allegations against Alice Huang baseless.

Huang resigned this week as Nvidia's CEO after damning reports that he had an affair with Alice Huang, who was an executive of Megaspeed, a cloud-computing company that is being eyed for possible facilitation of sending billions of dollars worth of Nvidia chips in violation of U.S. restrictions on those chips.

The scandal has rocked Nvidia and raised questions about whether the Commerce Department, which is led by Howard Lutnick, has been aggressive enough at blocking advanced chips from ending up in China.

During Friday's call, a reporter asked why Musk was going on the trip with Trump.

"I don't want to speak for the president on exactly why Elon's going," an official said.

"Obviously, he has tremendous business interests in the U.S.-China relationship. He's a big part of our national security," the official said. "There are numerous reasons, given the engagement that the president is going to have, where I think Elon would be of tremendous benefit, just even as an advisor to the president."

#102. Disney's 'Star Wars' is coming to Cosm domes ahead of 50th anniversary as part of multiyear film deal

On Thursday, the immersive entertainment technology company announced a new studio partnership with The Walt Disney Co. that will bring the original "Star Wars" to its domes early next year as part of a multiyear, multifilm collaboration.

The financial terms of the deal were not disclosed.

Cosm's massive concave screens will drop film fans into a galaxy far, far away starting in March, just ahead of the Academy Award-winning film's 50th anniversary in May.

"We are thrilled to add the Disney partnership," Alexis Scalice, head of Cosm Studios, told CNBC. "We do look for those opportunities of these like anniversary and commemorative moments. We look for those opportunities to celebrate with the fans, but also find this mutually beneficial partnership between both parties."

This will be Cosm's sixth theatrical collaboration with Hollywood, having already partnered with Warner Bros. to bring "The Matrix," "Willy Wonka & the Chocolate Factory," "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone" and "It" to its locations. The company is set to launch immersive screenings of Universal's "Shrek" in December.

Each Cosm dome has stadium-style seating, with booths and tables set on each level. The dome's width fills much of the viewers' periphery, giving the sensation that they are part of the onscreen environment.

In this case, "Star Wars" will be displayed in a large rectangle in the center of the dome while the background is transformed into the iconic planets of Tatooine and Yavin IV that are featured in the film as well as the infamous Death Star. Audiences will also see elements that will make it appear like they're traveling at lightspeed and getting trapped in an intergalactic trash compactor.

Cosm is working directly with studios Lucasfilm and Industrial Light and Magic as well as creative studio MakeMake to create the visuals and the sound mixing for the unique presentation of the film.

"We look for those moments where sound, music ... listening to Darth Vader's breath for the first time ... you feel those moments differently in an 87-foot diameter dome and 12k resolution with the sound system we have," Scalice said. "And it's one of the things that with experiential cinema and our technology that we were so excited and honestly privileged that we can take these defining moments for fans and work closely with our partners that doesn't really change the authenticity of it, it just enhances it in a different manner."

The premium experience will also be paired with a themed menu. Cosm has done this with other titles, such as "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone," serving guests throughout the screening with house-made shepherd's pie, pretzel wands with cheese dip and a box of Bertie Bott's Every Flavour Beans.

Cosm is trying to establish itself as a destination for fandom — both for sports and for film. It has a wide variety of live event offerings, which it telecasts using proprietary cameras mounted in arenas that broadcast back to the dome, as well as its expanding library of movies.

"What I love is that we really are kind of building these Cosm communities built on fandom," Scalice said. "It's the No. 1 foundation for our business."

And Star Wars is a powerful fandom. The franchise remains one of the top-ranking pieces of intellectual property across film, television and retail annually, and Disney is gearing up for a massive celebration of its semicentennial including merchandise launches, theatrical re-releases, its biannual Star Wars Celebration convention and collaborations with other brands.

Correction: This story has been updated to correct the spelling of Alexis Scalice's name.

#99. SpaceX launches Starship in key test flight of massive rocket

In a key test flight on Friday, SpaceX tried but failed to recover both stages of its massive Starship rocket.

The rocket lifted off from the company's South Texas launch facility at 4:47 p.m. local time, or 5:47 p.m. ET. SpaceX had to abort its plans to catch and reuse the booster, instead diverting it to splash into the Gulf of Mexico, where it burst into flames.

Test Flight 15, the most ambitious Starship mission to date, was the first time Elon Musk's company attempted to catch both the booster and upper stage, or spacecraft portion, of the massive rocket.

Earlier on Friday, SpaceX obtained authorization from the Federal Aviation Administration. Hours later, the FAA issued a nationwide ground stop after reporting communication problems affecting "multiple" air traffic control centers. The SpaceX license wasn't effected by the flight stoppage, a spokesperson for the FAA told CNBC.

The upper stage of the Starship continued its trajectory into orbit after the company failed to catch and salvage the booster. The rocket is expected to fly around the Earth fourteen times in total. If successful, SpaceX will land the upper stage back at its Starbase facility on Saturday.

Starship's success is critical to SpaceX and its ambitions to eventually lift cargo and people to the surface of the Moon and Mars. In the near-term, SpaceX needs to send more satellites into orbit to expands its Starlink satellite internet service, which is currently the company's profit center.

The company's constellation already includes over 11,000 active satellites in low Earth orbit. One Starship rocket has the capacity to carry up to 60 Starlink v3 satellites into orbit, the company has said, representing about 20 times the capacity of earlier Falcon 9 rockets.

In the previous Starship test flight, the company released 26 v3 satellites, and released another 26 in the Friday test flight, with the goal of "Starlink becoming 51% of the internet," SpaceX said in a post on X.

When it comes to launches, "SpaceX is likely a full decade ahead of peers on this front," William Blair analysts wrote in a note out on Friday. "Even if tonight's catch is unsuccessful, a delay by a few months is not that consequential."

Meanwhile, SpaceX is facing fresh criticism over restrictions imposed on its Starshield satellite internet service in Ukraine. Starshield is built on Starlink technology and is the military-tier of SpaceX's satellite internet service.

Earlier this week, Ukraine President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said his country's military forces had experienced limits on Starshield without warning again, harming their ability to defend against Russia's ongoing aerial attacks. Similar limits on Starshield in Ukraine were temporarily imposed last month.

Some U.S. senators have condemned the restrictions, and urged Congress to investigate SpaceX.

#96. Retailers are turning to entertainment to win shoppers’ attention

Legacy retailer Gap Inc. made an unconventional hire earlier this year: a chief entertainment officer.

It was a newly created role as the company tapped former Paramount executive Pam Kaufman to helm its entertainment, content and licensing platform. Gap has since rolled out major campaigns, partnering with bands like Just Your Type and building on the success of last year's ads with girl group Katseye.

"Beloved brands have value beyond the products they sell. Consumers identify with them, trust them and want to be part of their world," Kaufman told CNBC in a statement. "The key is authenticity: Every extension has to reflect what the brand stands for and give its fans a meaningful new way to experience it."

She said it's part of a broader goal to emphasize what Gap calls "fashiontainment," a strategy to blend retail and entertainment to better align with consumers. Gap is part of a growing cohort of retailers investing in entertainment and experiences both in-store and through exclusive online content to drive engagement.

"Brands are stories, and we are the storytellers," CEO Richard Dickson said at the Goldman Sachs Global Consumer and Retail conference in September. "I think you could see that come to life when you look at some of the work that we've done, putting our brands in the center of culture and cultural conversation."

In another blending of retail and media, Mattel made waves a few years ago for its "Barbie" movie, building on the intellectual property of a decades-old fan favorite and drawing in massive box office success. At the time, Dickson was president and chief operating officer of the company, witnessing firsthand the sectors' intersection before departing for Gap.

"I think the post-Covid consumer attitude around shopping certainly evolved a lot," said Bryan Eshelman, who leads the retail practice at consulting firm AlixPartners. "It has gone from shopping as a fulfillment task to shopping, in many instances, more as an entertainment form."

Between experiences in physical stores and media campaigns featuring exclusive content, Eshelman said, retailers are getting more creative about the ways they're driving engagement and marketing.

"Community, at the end of the day, is one of the key ways to drive loyalty, so I think retailers of all types are trying to tap into some sense of community, some sense of lifestyle, some sense of belonging that's beyond the product that they offer," he said.

Branded entertainment has been around for a long time. Procter & Gamble made headlines in the 1930s for helping to coin the term "soap opera," and companies have for decades created media campaigns to draw in customers.

But with the rise of social media, content creation and short-form offerings, experts said audiences are becoming hungrier than ever for something new and original.

The retail and entertainment intersection is happening at a time when intellectual property is becoming more valuable than ever, according to experts.

As consumers encounter more marketing than ever, the companies that will win the battle for attention are the ones that can "cut through all of the noise," said Alex Michael, senior managing director at investment firm LionTree.

"The hardest thing to find and to get is audience these days," Michael told CNBC. "IP, in that sense, is the most valuable thing out there, and only becoming more valuable because it's attached to audience."

At the same time, he said, the way IP is distributed is broadening to include video, audio, short-form, long-form and live event content. Consumers are also redefining their loyalty to brands, which means companies have to "play much earlier in the consumer journey" than they have in years past to secure their trust, he said.

The trend is also likely helped by executives who have worked in both retail and entertainment: Gap's Kaufman came from Paramount, for example, and Dickson and Skydance co-CEO Ynon Kreiz held top roles at Mattel.

"The gray areas have never been more pronounced as to what is classically defined as retail or media or entertainment or live events," Michael said. "People who have cross-sector expertise are increasingly more valuable because you can't be defined by a single vertical, especially as things change so quickly."

Still, there's room for newer players, especially with the studio landscape rapidly changing, said Sean Akaks, the CEO of SonderCo, an agency that partners brands with celebrities and entertainment properties.

"I think it's a really interesting time for nontraditional players to be making content because you're seeing this massive consolidation of these legacy entertainment companies," he said. "I think there are just more open lanes for independent creators and also brands and anyone outside the traditional studio system to create content."

That's where retailers might tap creators, Akaks said, because they can use their platforms to reach consumers directly.

"I do think there's a world where we're going to see creators and brands sort of co-creating IP that maybe they're owning together," he said.

Once those companies get consumers into the store, they're also leaning into experiential retail, making shopping a more immersive experience than it otherwise would be, like Dick's Sporting Goods offering interactive activities and simulators in its stores.

According to data from AlixPartners, companies investing in experiential retail such as Coach and Ralph Lauren saw close to 10% year-over-year increases in consumers' average basket size.

"The idea that retailers would potentially invest in some distributed electronic content to help drive traffic, and then when you get there, it's experiential — it really goes into the kinds of flywheels that entertainment companies are also trying to build and grow," said Jeff Goldstein, co-lead of the media and entertainment practice at AlixPartners.

Yet, the entertainment that retail brands are developing is only one piece in a larger puzzle to retain customers and attract new ones. Translating that into tangible sales growth isn't necessarily a given.

"The basics of having great product in stock at a transparent price that feels like value is not swept away by animatronics," AlixPartners' Eshelman said. "The fundamentals are still there, and if you're going to have a good retail proposition, you need to have that balance. You can't mask bad product at a bad price with no inventory with glitz and glamour."

#95. Eric Trump-backed robot soldier startup can't pay staff after founder borrowed $4.6 million from company to buy home

Foundation Future Industries, a maker of military humanoid robots advised and backed by Eric Trump, failed to pay employees last month as its cash reserves dwindled, according to people with direct knowledge of the matter.

Workers have yet to receive paychecks after San Francisco-based Foundation missed payroll on Oct. 30, said the people, who asked for anonymity to speak candidly about a private company.

The situation has particularly angered some inside the startup because its CEO and co-founder, Sankaet Pathak, borrowed $4.6 million from the company last year to buy a luxury home, money that could have been used to pay workers, the people said.

Public records show that an LLC that names Pathak as sole principal purchased a $4.6 million Sausalito, California, property with "sweeping, cinematic views" of the San Francisco Bay in October 2025.

A spokesman for Pathak acknowledged receiving a detailed list of the major points from this story on Thursday, but didn't respond to requests for comment. Pathak's previous startup, a fintech firm named Synapse, collapsed in 2024, leaving thousands of Americans locked out of their savings accounts.

Foundation's cash crunch comes at a precarious moment for the company, which Pathak has said is close to unveiling a next-generation, weapons-ready robot that will help the U.S. compete against Chinese rivals. At the same time, Pathak is seeking to raise hundreds of millions of dollars in funding at a $3 billion valuation.

The company has garnered headlines this year for Pathak's claims that his robots could soon take on battlefield tasks in Ukraine or patrol the U.S. border with Mexico. Broadcast interviews and articles about Foundation also include claims that the company has won $24 million in Pentagon contracts.

"What we're about to unveil in the next couple of months [is] going to be the strongest humanoid robot that exists anywhere in the world, including China," Pathak said in an April television interview.

That April interview introduced Eric Trump, the son of the U.S. president, as a Foundation investor and chief strategy advisor. It is unclear how large his stake is.

Eric Trump and his brother Donald Trump Jr. have invested in an array of defense firms, including makers of military drones and autonomous weapons. A July Washington Post investigation found that startups backed by investment vehicles linked to the brothers had secured at least $3.2 billion in government business.

"We have to win robotics in the United States of America," Eric Trump said in April. "When you go up and interact with these robots ... they fist bump you, they high five you, they follow your commands."

Eric Trump's involvement in Foundation drew the swift ire of Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., who asked whether Foundation won U.S. defense contracts because of nepotism.

"Eric Trump went on FOX to brag about winning ANOTHER multimillion-dollar contract from the Trump administration," Warren said in an April 23 social media post. "This looks like corruption in plain sight."

Eric Trump didn't respond to requests for comment. His spokesperson recently told the Financial Times that Eric Trump had "no involvement in Foundation's management, operations, strategic decisions or commercial activities, including the solicitation, negotiation, procurement or execution of any government contracts."

While the Eric Trump announcement created speculation within Foundation that a Democratic-led Congress may look to investigate the company, Foundation itself didn't actually win the $24 million in contracts, despite that assertion being widely reported across news outlets.

Instead, it acquired Florida-based Boardwalk Robotics in late 2024, and government research contracts won by that firm account for the claim, said a person with knowledge of the matter. Foundation used Boardwalk's robot Alex as the basis for its first robot, the Phantom MK-1, this person said.

Foundation's deal for Boardwalk included performance milestones for the robot that haven't been reached, said a separate person with direct knowledge of the deal.

In 2024, CNBC reported that letters Pathak sent to prospective investors cited an imminent investment and $300 million purchase order from General Motors, claims the automaker rejected.

Foundation co-founder Mike LeBlanc said at the time he was "embarrassed" about the marketing materials.

Insiders fret that Foundation could soon fold if Pathak doesn't secure fresh funding. Pathak has been seeking to raise hundreds of millions of dollars at a $3 billion valuation since at least January.

Pathak is Foundation's sole board member, a structure that has raised concerns among prospective investors about corporate governance, said the people familiar with the matter. He also owns most of the firm's shares.

That setup allowed Pathak to borrow $4.6 million last year, which he called a short-term loan at the time, the people said. Existing investors are aware of the loan through the company's financial disclosures, the people said.

"This is what happens in a company with no governance," said one of the people. "Are you just going to let it ride into the ground, and you ride off into the sunset?"

Foundation also ran out of cash for payroll in August, though employees only had to wait about two days to get paid after the company secured a bridge loan from an existing investor, the people said.

Pathak told employees this week in a memo that he was working on sourcing money to pay back wages.

The company was founded in April 2024 by Pathak, Tribe Capital CEO Arjun Sethi and LeBlanc, co-founder of Cobalt Robotics, a maker of autonomous security guards, according to a fundraising pitch at the time.

Foundation went on to hire dozens of robotics engineers and artificial intelligence experts, managing to lure some from Tesla and Amazon-owned autonomous vehicle company Zoox.

Before Foundation, Pathak was known as the founder of Synapse, a kind of middleman that enabled fintech firms like Mercury, Dave and YieldStreet to offer savings accounts and credit cards with the help of small community banks.

Synapse was a behind-the-scenes player until its collapse left more than 100,000 Americans with a combined $265 million in deposits locked out of their FDIC-backed accounts.

While some customers received partial payments during Synapse's bankruptcy proceedings, thousands remain without access to their money after more than two years. A bankruptcy trustee, the former FDIC chair Jelena McWilliams, said as much as $96 million in customer funds went missing.

The bankruptcy proceedings concluded last year without uncovering where the funds went; Pathak blamed a banking partner named Evolve, and Evolve blamed Synapse for the mess. U.S. regulators didn't intervene until the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau sued Synapse last year, opening up a $55 million fund for customer relief.

Pathak withdrew $320,000 in loans from Synapse as it spiraled toward bankruptcy in 2024 amid disputes over unmatched ledgers, according to the consultant and independent journalist Jason Mikula.

Federal prosecutors convened a grand jury to investigate possible criminal misconduct at Synapse, the Wall Street Journal reported last year. The WSJ report didn't mention Pathak specifically.

The controversy didn't dissuade Eric Trump from investing in Foundation and Pathak, who he called a friend in the April broadcast interview.

"I walked into his factory, and I couldn't believe what I saw," Eric Trump said. "I'm telling you, he's doing a phenomenal job."

#90. Flight disruptions continue after FAA lifts ground stop, some college football games delayed

Flight disruptions continued on Saturday and could extend through the weekend as airlines raced to recover from the Federal Aviation Administration's longest nationwide groundstop since 9/11, a measure the Trump administration blamed on a communications equipment failure in air traffic control.

More than 3,000 U.S. flights were canceled Saturday and close to 1,000 were delayed as of 5:45 a.m. ET, according to flight-tracking site FlightAware. That's on top of about 9,000 U.S. flight cancellations and more than 7,000 delays on Friday, though some disruptions were due to bad weather.

Part of the job of untangling from the ground stop includes getting aircraft where they need to be. The ground stop forced many planes to divert to other airports.

Airlines run one of the lightest schedules on Saturday and are deploying as many spare planes and crews as possible to get travelers and employees where they need to be as they're eager to start fresh for the upcoming Thanksgiving holiday period.

Around 3 million people would typically be scheduled to fly on Saturday, according to aviation data firm Cirium.

The FAA had halted departing U.S. flights around 4 p.m. on Friday, a ground stop that lasted more than four hours. Routers were overloaded with data and started giving air traffic control centers inconsistent information, so the U.S. issued the ground stop, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said at a news conference late Friday.

He blamed outdated U.S. infrastructure for the problem and said Congress needs to approve more money to overhaul the country's air traffic control. Aging equipment and a shortage of air traffic controllers have angered airline executives for years, who have complained about the cost of resulting disruptions.

It was the second time the FAA has issued a nationwide ground stop since the Sept. 11, 2001 terror attacks. Trump administration officials said it did not appear to be a cyberattack.

The U.S. has the world's busiest airspace.

"Before clearing flights to resume, we thoroughly tested our systems to confirm they were stable and that operations remain reliable," the FAA said late Friday.

The disruption also upended travel for NBA, NHL and college teams, which struggled to reach their destinations in time for games this weekend.

Duffy said college football teams traveling on charter flights could receive priority.

"I would encourage those football teams to reach out to the FAA to schedule their flight times and make sure they have games going off tomorrow," he said during a Friday press briefing.

The Southeastern Conference announced on X that some games had been moved to Sunday due to the flight disruption.

"We are in close communication with our member institutions to assess potential disruptions and coordinate appropriate responses," the SEC said in a statement shared on X.
